{"success": true, "name": "cron-job-design", "description": "Design, write, and debug Hermes cron jobs: output discipline, alert patterns, script-only vs agent jobs, dedup, and delivery targeting.", "tags": ["cron", "monitoring", "alerting", "scripting", "devops", "railway"], "related_skills": ["hermes-agent", "webhook-subscriptions"], "content": "---\nname: cron-job-design\ndescription: \"Design, write, and debug Hermes cron jobs: output discipline, alert patterns, script-only vs agent jobs, dedup, and delivery targeting.\"\nversion: 1.0.0\nauthor: hermes-agent\nplatforms: [linux]\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [cron, monitoring, alerting, scripting, devops, railway]\n    related_skills: [hermes-agent, webhook-subscriptions]\n---\n\n# Cron Job Design\n\nGuidance for writing cron scripts and cron job configurations that are genuinely useful — not noisy, not useless — for Hermes gateway deployments (Railway, Docker, local).\n\n---\n\n## Core principle: silent on OK, actionable on alert\n\n**DJ explicitly flagged raw status dumps as \"entirely useless.\"**\n\nA cron job that fires every N minutes and delivers verbose stats even when nothing is wrong is noise. The right model:\n\n- **Silent when OK.** Output nothing. The cron delivery system only sends output to Telegram (or wherever) if the script prints something.\n- **Use exactly three states when you do alert:**\n  1. **OK** — everything is fine; no DJ action.\n  2. **Warning** — something is off but not broken; say what is wrong and what DJ needs to do.\n  3. **Broken** — something is broken; say what is broken and what DJ needs to do now.\n- Alert only when something actionable is happening. The output must answer: *what broke, what context do I need to understand it, and what exact action does DJ need to take?* If the action is for Hermes, say `DJ action: none. Hermes should ...` explicitly.\n- Never send a raw status block — not memory/disk/process stats, not file ages, not check counts. Those belong in a status file written to disk for later inspection, not pushed to the user proactively.\n- Sanitize any upstream context that will be concatenated into a cron prompt or briefing. Invisible Unicode (especially `U+200B`, `U+200C`, `U+200D`, `U+2060`, and `U+FEFF`) in copied email/snippet text can trip the injection scanner before the agent runs.\n- Treat collector output as untrusted input even when it already looks like Markdown. Re-sanitize the exact files that feed the prompt after any fallback read or retry path.\n\n## Report jobs: daily + weekly, with truthful zeroes\n\nSpend-style report jobs are not health alerts. They should be designed as **explicit daily and weekly summaries** with stable cadence and unambiguous windows.\n\nRules:\n- **Daily and weekly should both exist** when the user asks for both. Do not assume a daily job covers the weekly need.\n- **Report the actual ledger result, even if it is zero.** If spend is `0.0000` or the pricing source is `included`, say that plainly instead of implying failure or activity.\n- **Do not infer work happened from the existence of a scheduled run.** A successful cron wakeup is not evidence that any spend events occurred.\n- **Call out pricing caveats explicitly.** If `estimated_cost_usd` is zero because the provider or pricing path is still unresolved, the report should surface that as a caveat, not hide it.\n- **Use the same source of truth for both cadence variants** so the daily and weekly numbers reconcile (same ledger, different window).\n- **If a report belongs with a health check, prefer a single combined daily wrapper over two separate Telegram messages.** The wrapper should run the health probe first, then the report, and send one scan-friendly message when healthy. Keep the dedicated report job only if it serves a different audience or cadence.\n\nFor spend reports, the primary question is: *what did the ledger record in the window?* Not: *did the job run?*\n\n---\n\n## Alert output format\n\nWhen there IS something DJ-actionable, the alert message should be:\n\n``​`\n⚠️ <System> needs DJ attention\nTime: <ISO UTC>\n\nWhat changed:\n- <issue 1>\n- <issue 2>\n\nUseful context:\n- <metric 1>: <value>\n- <metric 2>: <value>\n\n[Optional: Recent severe log lines:]\n<log tail>\n\n[Optional: Operational issue details:]\n- <detail>\n\nDJ action: <specific action required>\n``​`\n\nIf the recommended DJ action is `none`, do **not** label it \"needs attention.\" Either stay silent (preferred for recurring monitors) or label it clearly as internal follow-up, e.g. `ℹ️ <System> Hermes follow-up logged` with `DJ action: none.`\n\nKey rules:\n- Lead with emoji flag so it's scannable in Telegram.\n- \"What changed\" = the issues list, bullet-formatted.\n- \"Useful context\" = metrics only when the issue could be caused by resource pressure.\n- Never combine \"needs attention\" with \"DJ action: none.\" That is contradictory and DJ explicitly rejected it.\n\n## LLM-backed cron jobs: use the shared accounting helper\n\nWhen a cron job calls OpenRouter directly from a script, prefer the shared helper pattern used by Hermes-owned scripts instead of hand-rolling `requests.post(...)` plus ad hoc usage logging.\n\nGuideline:\n- centralize request + spend recording in a shared helper;\n- attach stable metadata like `project_slug`, `source`, `platform`, and a stage name;\n- verify the rollout by compiling the changed scripts and searching for lingering direct OpenRouter URL calls in the active script tree.\n\nThis keeps token tracking, cost estimation, and ledger attribution aligned across future projects.\n\n## Spend reports: use the session insights path, not the raw ledger\n\nFor Hermes daily/weekly spend reporting, the authoritative reporting source is the *session insights* path (`SessionDB` + `InsightsEngine`), not the narrower `agent.spend_ledger` table alone.\n\nWhy:\n- the raw ledger can show only a partial view of spend attribution;\n- `InsightsEngine.generate(days=...)` already expose

### tool

```text
{"content": "     1|# Hermes spend reporting\n     2|\n     3|This note captures the fix for daily/weekly spend briefing jobs.\n     4|\n     5|## Root cause\n     6|\n     7|A daily spend briefing originally used `agent.spend_ledger.summarize_spend(...)` over the raw `llm_usage_events` ledger. That path was useful for event-level attribution, but it underreported the full session-level spend picture the user expected.\n     8|\n     9|## Correct reporting source\n    10|\n    11|Use:\n    12|- `hermes_state.SessionDB`\n    13|- `agent.insights.InsightsEngine`\n    14|\n    15|Why:\n    16|- `InsightsEngine.generate(days=N)` already computes:\n    17|  - `overview.estimated_cost`\n    18|  - `overview.actual_cost`\n    19|  - session counts\n    20|  - token counts\n    21|  - included/unknown pricing session counts\n    22|  - model/platform breakdowns\n    23|- It is the same source used by the built-in `hermes insights` command.\n    24|- Caveat: `overview.actual_cost` is not always a trustworthy billed-cost source. If it is missing, null, or zero in a window that clearly contains paid usage, render billed spend as `unavailable` and reconcile with the provider’s billing API instead of printing `$0.0000`.\n    25|\n    26|## Recommended daily report fields\n    27|\n    28|- Estimated spend (last 24h)\n    29|- Actual billed spend (last 24h)\n    30|- Sessions (last 24h)\n    31|- Tokens (last 24h)\n    32|- Included sessions\n    33|- Unknown pricing sessions\n    34|- Top models\n    35|- Top platforms\n    36|- Project totals for the work done in the window\n    37|- Workflow/stage totals when available\n    38|\n    39|## Recommended weekly report fields\n    40|\n    41|- Estimated spend (last 7d)\n    42|- Actual billed spend (last 7d)\n    43|- Sessions (last 7d)\n    44|- Tokens (last 7d)\n    45|- Included sessions\n    46|- Unknown pricing sessions\n    47|- Top models\n    48|- Top platforms\n    49|- Project totals for the work done in the window\n    50|- Workflow/stage totals when available\n    51|\n    52|## Verification\n    53|\n    54|Useful local checks:\n    55|\n    56|``​`bash\n    57|python3 -m hermes_cli.main insights --days 1\n    58|python3 -m hermes_cli.main spend summary --days 1 --by provider\n    59|``​`\n    60|\n    61|The first command is the canonical report source for daily/weekly spend briefings.\n    62|The second command is useful for raw ledger comparison, but not as the primary user-facing spend report.\n    63|", "total_lines": 62, "file_size": 1982, "truncated": false, "is_binary": false, "is_image": false}
```

```text
{"content": "     1|# Cron failure triage\n     2|\n     3|Use this when a cron job reports `exit code 1` or delivers a failure alert.\n     4|\n     5|## Triage order\n     6|\n     7|1. **Find the wrapper log** for the exact cron run.\n     8|2. **Read the first traceback / first nonzero failing step.** Do not start with the tail.\n     9|3. **Identify the exact script and interpreter** the cron wrapper used.\n    10|4. **Reproduce the failing step with the same command line** in the live environment.\n    11|5. **Fix the code path first** when the failure is deterministic.\n    12|6. **If the failure is in the runtime path**, make the job robust via a local fallback or shared helper instead of duplicating logic in each script.\n    13|7. **Re-run the same command once** to verify the fix before declaring success.\n    14|\n    15|## Useful checks\n    16|\n    17|- Did the wrapper stop before the digest/report render step?\n    18|- Did the failure come from an import at process start?\n    19|- Is the job using the same interpreter as the cron wrapper?\n    20|- Is there a cleaner shared helper the job should use instead of hand-rolled code?\n    21|\n    22|## Goal\n    23|\n    24|Turn the failing cron into one of:\n    25|- silent when healthy\n    26|- actionable when broken\n    27|- deterministic enough that the same class of failure is less likely to recur\n    28|", "total_lines": 27, "file_size": 1162, "truncated": false, "is_binary": false, "is_image": false}
```

_tool_call_id: `call_eiHgT4ibpcrfskgwe4z3JcL6`_

### tool

```text
{"content": "     1|# Daily health + spend wrapper pattern\n     2|\n     3|Use this when a single Telegram delivery should combine the daily health check and the Hermes spend briefing.\n     4|\n     5|## Why\n     6|\n     7|- keeps the daily health update and spend snapshot in one scan-friendly message\n     8|- avoids a second separate spend notification when the spend section is only supporting context\n     9|- preserves the health check's actionable tone while still surfacing spend\n    10|- stays robust across ET/DST by using a date guard in the wrapper, not in cron alone\n    11|\n    12|## Pattern\n    13|\n    14|1. Run the health check first with forced reporting enabled.\n    15|2. Run the spend briefing next with forced reporting enabled.\n    16|3. Print the health section before the spend section.\n    17|4. Keep the wrapper script idempotent for the ET day.\n    18|5. Make the cron job `no_agent: true` so the wrapper output is the delivery.\n    19|\n    20|## Environment flags used\n    21|\n    22|- `CHIEF_HEALTH_ALWAYS_REPORT=1`\n    23|- `HERMES_SPEND_REPORT_FORCE=1`\n    24|- optional wrapper-only guard like `HERMES_DAILY_HEALTH_SPEND_FORCE=1` for debugging\n    25|\n    26|## Practical notes\n    27|\n    28|- Use a separate state file under `/opt/data/spend-monitor/` or similar for the ET-date dedupe key.\n    29|- Do not rely on raw cron timing alone for 5AM ET jobs; use UTC candidate wakeups plus an in-script ET hour/date check.\n    30|- Keep the wrapper lightweight: shell/Python orchestration only, no LLM turn unless the job is actually a summary job that needs it.\n    31|- If the combined output is meant for Telegram, make it self-identifying and concise; avoid wrapper boilerplate and job metadata.\n    32|\n    33|## Example sequence\n    34|\n    35|``​`text\n    36|Health check\n    37|  -> spend briefing\n    38|  -> one Telegram message\n    39|``​`\n    40|\n    41|## Verification\n    42|\n    43|- run the wrapper once with its force flag\n    44|- confirm the health section renders first\n    45|- confirm the spend section includes the expected 24h and 7d windows\n    46|- confirm the cron job is scheduled as a single delivery, not two separate jobs\n    47|", "total_lines": 46, "file_size": 1844, "truncated": false, "is_binary": false, "is_image": false}
```

timedelta(days=days)\n    cutoff_epoch = cutoff.timestamp()\n\n    if not SPEND_DB.exists():\n        raise FileNotFoundError(f\"Spend database not found: {SPEND_DB}\")\n\n    with sqlite3.connect(SPEND_DB) as con:\n        con.row_factory = sqlite3.Row\n        rows = con.execute(\n            \"\"\"\n            SELECT *\n            FROM llm_usage_events\n            WHERE created_at >= ?\n            ORDER BY created_at ASC\n            \"\"\",\n            (cutoff_epoch,),\n        ).fetchall()\n\n    overview = {\n        \"estimated_cost\": 0.0,\n        \"actual_cost\": None,\n        \"total_tokens\": 0,\n        \"session_count\": 0,\n        \"included_sessions\": 0,\n        \"unknown_pricing_sessions\": 0,\n    }\n    if not rows:\n        return {\"overview\": overview, \"models\": [], \"workstreams\": [], \"channels\": []}\n\n    sessions: set[str] = set()\n    included_sessions: set[str] = set()\n    unknown_sessions: set[str] = set()\n\n    model_totals: dict[str, dict[str, Any]] = defaultdict(lambda: {\"cost\": 0.0, \"tokens\": 0, \"count\": 0, \"statuses\": set()})\n    workstream_totals: dict[str, dict[str, Any]] = defaultdict(lambda: {\"cost\": 0.0, \"tokens\": 0, \"count\": 0, \"models\": defaultdict(lambda: {\"cost\": 0.0, \"tokens\": 0, \"count\": 0})})\n    channel_totals: dict[str, dict[str, Any]] = defaultdict(lambda: {\"cost\": 0.0, \"tokens\": 0, \"count\": 0})\n\n    for row in rows:\n        meta = _load_metadata(row[\"metadata_json\"])\n        session_id = str(row[\"session_id\"] or \"\")\n        if session_id:\n            sessions.add(session_id)\n\n        cost = _safe_float(row[\"estimated_cost_usd\"])\n        tokens = _safe_int(row[\"total_tokens\"])\n        overview[\"estimated_cost\"] += cost\n        o

### assistant

```text
Fixed.

*Why it failed:* the cron script was importing `agent.spend_ledger` and `hermes_constants`, but those modules are **not on the cron runtime’s Python path** here, so it died immediately with `ModuleNotFoundError`.

*What I changed:*
- Added a local `spend_report_helper.py` that:
  - tries the richer Hermes session-insights path when available
  - falls back to reading `/opt/data/spend.db` directly when those Hermes modules aren’t importable
- Updated both:
  - `hermes_spend_briefing.py`
  - `hermes_weekly_spend_briefing.py`
  to use that helper
- Removed the hard dependency on `hermes_constants` by resolving `HERMES_HOME` locally, with `~/.hermes` as fallback
- Mirrored the same fix into the backup copy under `railway-opt-data/scripts/`

*Verified:*
- both scripts compile cleanly
- both scripts now run successfully in this environment
- force-running them produces a daily and weekly spend briefing instead of crashing

If you want, I can also make the report output a bit cleaner for Telegram, since the fallback ledger only has `cron/cli` style labels in this environment.
```

{"success": true, "name": "systematic-debugging", "description": "4-phase root cause debugging: understand bugs before fixing.", "tags": ["debugging", "troubleshooting", "problem-solving", "root-cause", "investigation"], "related_skills": ["test-driven-development", "writing-plans", "subagent-driven-development"], "content": "---\nname: systematic-debugging\ndescription: \"4-phase root cause debugging: understand bugs before fixing.\"\nversion: 1.1.0\nauthor: Hermes Agent (adapted from obra/superpowers)\nlicense: MIT\nplatforms: [linux, macos, windows]\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [debugging, troubleshooting, problem-solving, root-cause, investigation]\n    related_skills: [test-driven-development, writing-plans, subagent-driven-development]\n---\n\n# Systematic Debugging\n\n## Overview\n\nRandom fixes waste time and create new bugs. Quick patches mask underlying issues.\n\n**Core principle:** ALWAYS find root cause before attempting fixes. Symptom fixes are failure.\n\n**Violating the letter of this process is violating the spirit of debugging.**\n\n## The Iron Law\n\n``​`\nNO FIXES WITHOUT ROOT CAUSE INVESTIGATION FIRST\n``​`\n\nIf you haven't completed Phase 1, you cannot propose fixes.\n\n## When to Use\n\nUse for ANY technical issue:\n- Test failures\n- Bugs in production\n- Unexpected behavior\n- Performance problems\n- Build failures\n- Integration issues\n\n**Use this ESPECIALLY when:**\n- Under time pressure (emergencies make guessing tempting)\n- \"Just one quick fix\" seems obvious\n- You've already tried multiple fixes\n- Previous fix didn't work\n- You don't fully understand the issue\n\n**Don't skip when:**\n- Issue seems simple (simple bugs have root causes too)\n- You're in a hurry (rushing guarantees rework)\n- Someone wants it fixed NOW (systematic is faster than thrashing)\n\n**Important interaction rule:** if the user says the product is broken or a bug is obvious, do *not* ask permission to fix it. Start investigation and repair immediately. Only ask a clarifying question when the scope is genuinely ambiguous or the action would be destructive.\n\n## The Four Phases\n\nYou MUST complete each phase before proceeding to the next.\n\n---\n\n## Phase 1: Root Cause Investigation\n\n**BEFORE attempting ANY fix:**\n\n### 1. Read Error Messages Carefully\n\n- Don't skip past errors or warnings\n- They often contain the exact solution\n- Read stack traces completely\n- Note line numbers, file paths, error codes\n\n**Action:** Use `read_file` on the relevant source files. Use `search_files` to find the error string in the codebase.\n\n### 2. Reproduce Consistently\n\n- Can you trigger it reliably?\n- What are the exact steps?\n- Does it happen every time?\n- If not reproducible → gather more data, don't guess\n\n**Action:** Use the `terminal` tool to run the failing test or trigger the bug:\n\n``​`bash\n# Run specific failing test\npytest tests/test_module.py::test_name -v\n\n# Run with verbose output\npytest tests/test_module.py -v --tb=long\n``​`\n\n### 3. Check Recent Changes\n\n- What changed that could cause this?\n- Git diff, recent commits\n- New dependencies, config changes\n\n**Action:**\n\n``​`bash\n# Recent commits\ngit log --oneline -10\n\n# Uncommitted changes\ngit diff\n\n# Changes in specific file\ngit log -p --follow src/problematic_file.py | head -100\n``​`\n\n### 4. Gather Evidence in Multi-Component Systems\n\n**WHEN system has multiple components (API → service → database, CI → build → deploy):**\n\n**BEFORE proposing fixes, add diagnostic instrumentation:**\n\nFor EACH component boundary:\n- Log what data enters the component\n- Log what data exits the component\n- Verify environment/config propagation\n- Check state at each layer\n\nRun once to gather evidence showing WHERE it breaks.\nTHEN analyze evidence to identify the failing component.\nTHEN investigate that specific component.\n\n### 5. Trace Data Flow\n\n**WHEN error is deep in the call stack:**\n\n- Where does the bad value originate?\n- What called this function with the bad value?\n- Keep tracing upstream until you find the source\n- Fix at the source, not at the symptom\n\n**Action:** Use `search_files` to trace references:\n\n``​`python\n# Find where the function is called\nsearch_files(\"function_name(\", path=\"src/\", file_glob=\"*.py\")\n\n# Find where the variable is set\nsearch_files(\"variable_name\\\\s*=\", path=\"src/\", file_glob=\"*.py\")\n``​`\n\n### 6. For Web Apps, Verify the Deployed Client Code\n\nWhen the symptom is \"button does nothing\", \"form submits nowhere\", or a browser-side action appears inert:\n\n- First confirm whether the backend/API works with a direct request.\n- Then inspect the deployed HTML/JS assets to verify the expected client handler is actually deployed.\n- Do not infer client interactivity from server-rendered HTML alone; React/Next event handlers are in JS chunks, not literal `onSubmit` attributes in the HTML.\n- Check for uncommitted/staged-only local changes if production is missing code you expected to be live.\n\nExample probe:\n\n``​`bash\npython3 - <<'PY'\nimport re, urllib.request\nbase='https://example.app'\nhtml=urllib.request.urlopen(base+'/login', timeout=30).read().decode('utf-8','replace')\nfor src in re.findall(r'<script[^>]+src=\"([^\"]+\\.js)\"', html):\n    js=urllib.request.urlopen(base+src, timeout=30).read().decode('utf-8','replace')\n    if '/api/auth/login' in js or 'window.location.assign' in js:\n        print('found login handler in', src)\nPY\n``​`\n\n### 7. For DB-Backed API 500s, Inspect Provider Logs and Normalize Payloads\n\nWhen a form displays a generic save failure but the API returns a blank 500:\n\n- Reproduce the exact API request with an authenticated session and record status/body.\n- Pull provider/runtime logs before patching; ORMs often put the real validation error there when the HTTP response is empty.\n- Compare frontend payload shape, validation schema, and ORM model field types.\n- Do not pass raw validated brow

2. monitor detects it

{"success": true, "name": "github-auth", "description": "GitHub auth setup: HTTPS tokens, SSH keys, gh CLI login.", "tags": ["GitHub", "Authentication", "Git", "gh-cli", "SSH", "Setup"], "related_skills": ["github-pr-workflow", "github-code-review", "github-issues", "github-repo-management"], "content": "---\nname: github-auth\ndescription: \"GitHub auth setup: HTTPS tokens, SSH keys, gh CLI login.\"\nversion: 1.1.0\nauthor: Hermes Agent\nlicense: MIT\nplatforms: [linux, macos, windows]\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [GitHub, Authentication, Git, gh-cli, SSH, Setup]\n    related_skills: [github-pr-workflow, github-code-review, github-issues, github-repo-management]\n---\n\n# GitHub Authentication Setup\n\nThis skill sets up authentication so the agent can work with GitHub repositories, PRs, issues, and CI. It covers two paths:\n\n- **`git` (always available)** — uses HTTPS personal access tokens or SSH keys\n- **`gh` CLI (if installed)** — richer GitHub API access with a simpler auth flow\n\n## Detection Flow\n\nWhen a user asks you to work with GitHub, run this check first:\n\n``​`bash\n# Check what's available\ngit --version\ngh --version 2>/dev/null || echo \"gh not installed\"\n\n# Check if already authenticated\ngh auth status 2>/dev/null || echo \"gh not authenticated\"\ngit config --global credential.helper 2>/dev/null || echo \"no git credential helper\"\n``​`\n\n**Decision tree:**\n1. If `gh auth status` shows authenticated → you're good, use `gh` for everything\n2. If `gh` is installed but not authenticated → use \"gh auth\" method below\n3. If `gh` is not installed → use \"git-only\" method below (no sudo needed)\n\n---\n\n## Method 1: Git-Only Authentication (No gh, No sudo)\n\nThis works on any machine with `git` installed. No root access needed.\n\n### Option A: HTTPS with Personal Access Token (Recommended)\n\nThis is the most portable method — works everywhere, no SSH config needed.\n\n**Step 1: Create a personal access token**\n\nTell the user to go to: **https://github.com/settings/tokens**\n\n- Click \"Generate new token (classic)\"\n- Give it a name like \"hermes-agent\"\n- Select scopes:\n  - `repo` (full repository access — read, write, push, PRs)\n  - `workflow` (trigger and manage GitHub Actions)\n  - `read:org` (if working with organization repos)\n- Set expiration (90 days is a good default)\n- Copy the token — it won't be shown again\n\n**Step 2: Configure git to store the token**\n\n``​`bash\n# Set up the credential helper to cache credentials\n# \"store\" saves to ~/.git-credentials in plaintext (simple, persistent)\ngit config --global credential.helper store\n\n# Now do a test operation that triggers auth — git will prompt for credentials\n# Username: <their-github-username>\n# Password: REDACTED the personal access token, NOT their GitHub password>\ngit ls-remote https://github.com/<their-username>/<any-repo>.git\n``​`\n\nAfter entering credentials once, they're saved and reused for all future operations.\n\n**Alternative: cache helper (credentials expire from memory)**\n\n``​`bash\n# Cache in memory for 8 hours (28800 seconds) instead of saving to disk\ngit config --global credential.helper 'cache --timeout=28800'\n``​`\n\n**Alternative: set the token directly in the remote URL (per-repo)**\n\n``​`bash\n# Embed token in the remote URL (avoids credential prompts entirely)\ngit remote set-url origin https://<username>:<token>@github.com/<owner>/<repo>.git\n``​`\n\n**Step 3: Configure git identity**\n\n``​`bash\n# Required for commits — set name and email\ngit config --global user.name \"Their Name\"\ngit config --global user.email \"[REDACTED_EMAIL]\"\n``​`\n\n**Step 4: Verify**\n\n``​`bash\n# Test push access (this should work without any prompts now)\ngit ls-remote https://github.com/<their-username>/<any-repo>.git\n\n# Verify identity\ngit config --global user.name\ngit config --global user.email\n``​`\n\n### Option B: SSH Key Authentication\n\nGood for users who prefer SSH or already have keys set up.\n\n**Step 1: Check for existing SSH keys**\n\n``​`bash\nls -la ~/.ssh/id_*.pub 2>/dev/null || echo \"No SSH keys found\"\n``​`\n\n**Step 2: Generate a key if needed**\n\n``​`bash\n# Generate an ed25519 key (modern, secure, fast)\nssh-keygen -t ed25519 -C \"[REDACTED_EMAIL]\" -f ~/.ssh/id_ed25519 -N \"\"\n\n# Display the public key for them to add to GitHub\ncat ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub\n``​`\n\nTell the user to add the public key at: **https://github.com/settings/keys**\n- Click \"New SSH key\"\n- Paste the public key content\n- Give it a title like \"hermes-agent-<machine-name>\"\n\n**Step 3: Test the connection**\n\n``​`bash\nssh -T [REDACTED_EMAIL]\n# Expected: \"Hi <username>! You've successfully authenticated...\"\n``​`\n\n**Step 4: Configure git to use SSH for GitHub**\n\n``​`bash\n# Rewrite HTTPS GitHub URLs to SSH automatically\ngit config --global url.\"[REDACTED_EMAIL]:\".insteadOf \"https://github.com/\"\n``​`\n\n**Step 5: Configure git identity**\n\n``​`bash\ngit config --global user.name \"Their Name\"\ngit config --global user.email \"[REDACTED_EMAIL]\"\n``​`\n\n---\n\n## Method 2: gh CLI Authentication\n\nIf `gh` is installed, it handles both API access and git credentials in one step.\n\n### Interactive Browser Login (Desktop)\n\n``​`bash\ngh auth login\n# Select: GitHub.com\n# Select: HTTPS\n# Authenticate via browser\n``​`\n\n### Token-Based Login (Headless / SSH Servers)\n\n### Token-Based Login (Headless / SSH Servers)\n\n``​`bash\necho \"<THEIR_TOKEN>\" | gh auth login --with-token\n\n# Set up git credentials through gh\ngh auth setup-git\n``​`\n\n**Headless/PTY fallback rule:** In Telegram, CI, SSH, or other bridged terminals, interactive `gh auth login --web` can stall on prompts such as protocol selection or \"Authenticate Git with your GitHub credentials?\". Do not keep restarting device-login loops. Try the web/device flow once; if prompt input is unreliable or the device code expires, kill stale `gh auth login` waiters, verify `gh auth status`, and switch

{"content": "     1|#!/usr/bin/env bash\r\n     2|# GitHub environment detection helper for Hermes Agent skills.\r\n     3|#\r\n     4|# Usage (via terminal tool):\r\n     5|#   source skills/github/github-auth/scripts/gh-env.sh\r\n     6|#\r\n     7|# After sourcing, these variables are set:\r\n     8|#   GH_AUTH_METHOD  - \"gh\", \"curl\", or \"none\"\r\n     9|#   GITHUB_TOKEN    - personal access token (set if method is \"curl\")\r\n    10|#   GH_USER         - GitHub username\r\n    11|#   GH_OWNER        - repo owner  (only if inside a git repo with a github remote)\r\n    12|#   GH_REPO         - repo name   (only if inside a git repo with a github remote)\r\n    13|#   GH_OWNER_REPO   - owner/repo  (only if inside a git repo with a github remote)\r\n    14|\r\n    15|# --- Auth detection ---\r\n    16|\r\n    17|GH_AUTH_METHOD=\"none\"\r\n    18|GITHUB_TOKEN=REDACTED    19|GH_USER=\"\"\r\n    20|\r\n    21|if command -v gh &>/dev/null && gh auth status &>/dev/null 2>&1; then\r\n    22|    GH_AUTH_METHOD=\"gh\"\r\n    23|    GH_USER=$(gh api user --jq '.login' 2>/dev/null)\r\n    24|elif [ -n \"$GITHUB_TOKEN\" ]; then\r\n    25|    GH_AUTH_METHOD=\"curl\"\r\n    26|elif [ -f \"$HOME/.hermes/.env\" ] && grep -q \"^GITHUB_TOKEN=REDACTED \"$HOME/.hermes/.env\" 2>/dev/null; then\r\n    27|    GITHUB_TOKEN=REDACTED \"^GITHUB_TOKEN=REDACTED \"$HOME/.hermes/.env\" | head -1 | cut -d= -f2 | tr -d '\\n\\r')\r\n    28|    if [ -n \"$GITHUB_TOKEN\" ]; then\r\n    29|        GH_AUTH_METHOD=\"curl\"\r\n    30|    fi\r\n    31|elif [ -f \"$HOME/.git-credentials\" ] && grep -q \"github.com\" \"$HOME/.git-credentials\" 2>/dev/null; then\r\n    32|    GITHUB_TOKEN=REDACTED \"github.com\" \"$HOME/.git-credentials\" | head -1 | sed 's|https://[^:***@]*\\)@.*|\\1|')\r\n    33|    if [ -n \"$GITHUB_TOKEN\" ]; then\r\n    34|        GH_AUTH_METHOD=\"curl\"\r\n    35|    fi\r\n    36|fi\r\n    37|\r\n    38|# Resolve username for curl method\r\n    39|if [ \"$GH_AUTH_METHOD\" = \"curl\" ] && [ -z \"$GH_USER\" ]; then\r\n    40|    GH_USER=$(curl -s -H \"Authorization: token $GITHUB_TOKEN\" \\\r\n    41|        https://api.github.com/user 2>/dev/null \\\r\n    42|        | python3 -c \"import sys,json; print(json.load(sys.stdin).get('login',''))\" 2>/dev/null)\r\n    43|fi\r\n    44|\r\n    45|# --- Repo detection (if inside a git repo with a GitHub remote) ---\r\n    46|\r\n    47|GH_OWNER=\"\"\r\n    48|GH_REPO=\"\"\r\n    49|GH_OWNER_REPO=\"\"\r\n    50|\r\n    51|_remote_url=$(git remote get-url origin 2>/dev/null)\r\n    52|if [ -n \"$_remote_url\" ] && echo \"$_remote_url\" | grep -q \"github.com\"; then\r\n    53|    GH_OWNER_REPO=$(echo \"$_remote_url\" | sed -E 's|.*github\\.com[:/]||; s|\\.git$||')\r\n    54|    GH_OWNER=$(echo \"$GH_OWNER_REPO\" | cut -d/ -f1)\r\n    55|    GH_REPO=$(echo \"$GH_OWNER_REPO\" | cut -d/ -f2)\r\n    56|fi\r\n    57|unset _remote_url\r\n    58|\r\n    59|# --- Summary ---\r\n    60|\r\n    61|echo \"GitHub Auth: $GH_AUTH_METHOD\"\r\n    62|[ -n \"$GH_USER\" ]       && echo \"User: $GH_USER\"\r\n    63|[ -n \"$GH_OWNER_REPO\" ] && echo \"Repo: $GH_OWNER_REPO\"\r\n    64|[ \"$GH_AUTH_METHOD\" = \"none\" ] && echo \"⚠ Not authenticated — see github-auth skill\"\r\n    65|\r\n    66|export GH_AUTH_METHOD GITHUB_TOKEN GH_USER GH_OWNER GH_REPO GH_OWNER_REPO\r\n    67|", "total_lines": 66, "file_size": 2640, "truncated": false, "is_binary": false, "is_image": false}

### user

```text
Review the conversation above and update the skill library. Be ACTIVE — most sessions produce at least one skill update, even if small. A pass that does nothing is a missed learning opportunity, not a neutral outcome.

Target shape of the library: CLASS-LEVEL skills, each with a rich SKILL.md and a `references/` directory for session-specific detail. Not a long flat list of narrow one-session-one-skill entries. This shapes HOW you update, not WHETHER you update.

Signals to look for (any one of these warrants action):
  • User corrected your style, tone, format, legibility, or verbosity. Frustration signals like 'stop doing X', 'this is too verbose', 'don't format like this', 'why are you explaining', 'just give me the answer', 'you always do Y and I hate it', or an explicit 'remember this' are FIRST-CLASS skill signals, not just memory signals. Update the relevant skill(s) to embed the preference so the next session starts already knowing.
  • User corrected your workflow, approach, or sequence of steps. Encode the correction as a pitfall or explicit step in the skill that governs that class of task.
  • Non-trivial technique, fix, workaround, debugging path, or tool-usage pattern emerged that a future session would benefit from. Capture it.
  • A skill that got loaded or consulted this session turned out to be wrong, missing a step, or outdated. Patch it NOW.

Preference order — prefer the earliest action that fits, but do pick one when a signal above fired:
  1. UPDATE A CURRENTLY-LOADED SKILL. Look back through the conversation for skills the user loaded via /skill-name or you read via skill_view. If any of them covers the territory of the new learning, PATCH that one first. It is the skill that was in play, so it's the right one to extend.
  2. UPDATE AN EXISTING UMBRELLA (via skills_list + skill_view). If no loaded skill fits but an existing class-level skill does, patch it. Add a subsection, a pitfall, or broaden a trigger.
  3. ADD A SUPPORT FILE under an existing umbrella. Skills can be packaged with three kinds of support files — use the right directory per kind:
     • `references/<topic>.md` — session-specific detail (error transcripts, reproduction recipes, provider quirks) AND condensed knowledge banks: quoted research, API docs, external authoritative excerpts, or domain notes you found while working on the problem. Write it concise and for the value of the task, not as a full mirror of upstream docs.
     • `templates/<name>.<ext>` — starter files meant to be copied and modified (boilerplate configs, scaffolding, a known-good example the agent can `reproduce with modifications`).
     • `scripts/<name>.<ext>` — statically re-runnable actions the skill can invoke directly (verification scripts, fixture generators, deterministic probes, anything the agent should run rather than hand-type each time).
     Add support files via skill_manage action=write_file with file_path starting 'references/', 'templates/', or 'scripts/'. The umbrella's SKILL.md should gain a one-line pointer to any new support file so future agents know it exists.
  4. CREATE A NEW CLASS-LEVEL UMBRELLA SKILL when no existing skill covers the class. The name MUST be at the class level. The name MUST NOT be a specific PR number, error string, feature codename, library-alone name, or 'fix-X / debug-Y / audit-Z-today' session artifact. If the proposed name only makes sense for today's task, it's wrong — fall back to (1), (2), or (3).

User-preference embedding (important): when the user expressed a style/format/workflow preference, the update belongs in the SKILL.md body, not just in memory. Memory captures 'who the user is and what the current situation and state of your operations are'; skills capture 'how to do this class of task for this user'. When they complain about how you handled a task, the skill that governs that task needs to carry the lesson.

If you notice two existing skills that overlap, note it in your reply — the background curator handles consolidation at scale.

Do NOT capture (these become persistent self-imposed constraints that bite you later when the environment changes):
  • Environment-dependent failures: missing binaries, fresh-install errors, post-migration path mismatches, 'command not found', unconfigured credentials, uninstalled packages. The user can fix these — they are not durable rules.
  • Negative claims about tools or features ('browser tools do not work', 'X tool is broken', 'cannot use Y from execute_code'). These harden into refusals the agent cites against itself for months after the actual problem was fixed.
  • Session-specific transient errors that resolved before the conversation ended. If retrying worked, the lesson is the retry pattern, not the original failure.
  • One-off task narratives. A user asking 'summarize today's market' or 'analyze this PR' is not a class of work that warrants a skill.

If a tool failed because of setup state, capture the FIX (install command, config step, env var to set) under an existing setup or troubleshooting skill — never 'this tool does not work' as a standalone constraint.

'Nothing to save.' is a real option but should NOT be the default. If the session ran smoothly with no corrections and produced no new technique, just say 'Nothing to save.' and stop. Otherwise, act.
```

Read `cron/jobs.json` to map job IDs to script names and human-readable job names.\n2. Scan recent cron output files for `Status: script failed` / `Status: failed`.\n3. Track a monotonic `last_scan_epoch` so each run only considers new output files.\n4. Keep a seen-failures set keyed by output path or fingerprint so repeated scans stay quiet.\n5. Apply narrow repairs only for known, deterministic classes.\n6. Verify the repaired script with `bash -n` or `py_compile` before considering the fix complete.\n\n## Safe auto-repair examples\n\n- Import/path failures in cron Python scripts that can be corrected by adding a local fallback helper.\n- Script copies in mirrored repos that must be kept in sync with the primary script tree.\n- Rewriting a broken authenticated Git URL to use runtime env expansion like `${GITHUB_TOKEN}`.\n\n## Pitfalls\n\n- Do not rescan the entire cron history every run; use a `last_scan_epoch` or equivalent cursor.\n- Do not auto-repair ambiguous failures.\n- Do not emit repeated alerts for the same unresolved failure.\n- Do not hardcode secret values in the fix; use env expansion and keep credentials out of output.\n\n## Verification\n\n- `bash -n` for shell scripts\n- `python3 -m py_compile` for Python scripts\n- Run the repaired script once in the same execution style as the cron wrapper when feasible\n"}

{"success": true, "name": "cron-job-design", "description": "Design, write, and debug Hermes cron jobs: output discipline, alert patterns, script-only vs agent jobs, dedup, and delivery targeting.", "tags": ["cron", "monitoring", "alerting", "scripting", "devops", "railway"], "related_skills": ["hermes-agent", "webhook-subscriptions"], "content": "---\nname: cron-job-design\ndescription: \"Design, write, and debug Hermes cron jobs: output discipline, alert patterns, script-only vs agent jobs, dedup, and delivery targeting.\"\nversion: 1.0.0\nauthor: hermes-agent\nplatforms: [linux]\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [cron, monitoring, alerting, scripting, devops, railway]\n    related_skills: [hermes-agent, webhook-subscriptions]\n---\n\n# Cron Job Design\n\nGuidance for writing cron scripts and cron job configurations that are genuinely useful — not noisy, not useless — for Hermes gateway deployments (Railway, Docker, local).\n\n---\n\n## Core principle: silent on OK, actionable on alert\n\n**DJ explicitly flagged raw status dumps as \"entirely useless.\"**\n\nA cron job that fires every N minutes and delivers verbose stats even when nothing is wrong is noise. The right model:\n\n- **Silent when OK.** Output nothing. The cron delivery system only sends output to Telegram (or wherever) if the script prints something.\n- **Use exactly three states when you do alert:**\n  1. **OK** — everything is fine; no DJ action.\n  2. **Warning** — something is off but not broken; say what is wrong and what DJ needs to do.\n  3. **Broken** — something is broken; say what is broken and what DJ needs to do now.\n- Alert only when something actionable is happening. The output must answer: *what broke, what context do I need to understand it, and what exact action does DJ need to take?* If the action is for Hermes, say `DJ action: none. Hermes should ...` explicitly.\n- Never send a raw status block — not memory/disk/process stats, not file ages, not check counts. Those belong in a status file written to disk for later inspection, not pushed to the user proactively.\n- Sanitize any upstream context that will be concatenated into a cron prompt or briefing. Invisible Unicode (especially `U+200B`, `U+200C`, `U+200D`, `U+2060`, and `U+FEFF`) in copied email/snippet text can trip the injection scanner before the agent runs.\n- Treat collector output as untrusted input even when it already looks like Markdown. Re-sanitize the exact files that feed the prompt after any fallback read or retry path.\n\n## Report jobs: daily + weekly, with truthful zeroes\n\nSpend-style report jobs are not health alerts. They should be designed as **explicit daily and weekly summaries** with stable cadence and unambiguous windows.\n\nRules:\n- **Daily and weekly should both exist** when the user asks for both. Do not assume a daily job covers the weekly need.\n- **Report the actual ledger result, even if it is zero.** If spend is `0.0000` or the pricing source is `included`, say that plainly instead of implying failure or activity.\n- **Do not infer work happened from the existence of a scheduled run.** A successful cron wakeup is not evidence that any spend events occurred.\n- **Call out pricing caveats explicitly.** If `estimated_cost_usd` is zero because the provider or pricing path is still unresolved, the report should surface that as a caveat, not hide it.\n- **Use the same source of truth for both cadence variants** so the daily and weekly numbers reconcile (same ledger, different window).\n- **If a report belongs with a health check, prefer a single combined daily wrapper over two separate Telegram messages.** The wrapper should run the health probe first, then the report, and send one scan-friendly message when healthy. Keep the dedicated report job only if it serves a different audience or cadence.\n\nFor spend reports, the primary question is: *what did the ledger record in the window?* Not: *did the job run?*\n\n---\n\n## Auto-repair monitor pattern\n\nFor recurring, deterministic cron failures, prefer a *self-heal monitor* over repeated manual triage.\n\nUse it when:\n- the failure signature is exact and stable;\n- the fix is small and safe to apply automatically;\n- you can verify the patch with a compile or dry-run step;\n- the same class of failure is likely to recur.\n\nWorkflow:\n1. Scan recent cron output artifacts for new failures.\n2. Keep a last-scan timestamp and seen-failure set so old failures are not reprocessed.\n3. If the signature matches a known narrow class, patch the live script in place.\n4. Re-run a compile or dry-run verification immediately.\n5. Emit a concise Telegram message only if a fix was applied or human attention is still needed.\n\nPitfalls:\n- Don’t auto-fix ambiguous auth/permission issues.\n- Don’t widen the patch to unrelated cleanup.\n- Don’t spam repeated alerts for the same failure unless the failure is genuinely new.\n- If the script exists in more than one mirrored location, patch every runtime copy, not just the source-of-truth checkout.\n\nSupport file:\n- `references/self-heal-monitor.md`\n\n## Alert output format\n\nWhen there IS something DJ-actionable, the alert message should be:\n\n``​`\n⚠️ <System> needs DJ attention\nTime: <ISO UTC>\n\nWhat changed:\n- <issue 1>\n- <issue 2>\n\nUseful context:\n- <metric 1>: <value>\n- <metric 2>: <value>\n\n[Optional: Recent severe log lines:]\n<log tail>\n\n[Optional: Operational issue details:]\n- <detail>\n\nDJ action: <specific action required>\n``​`\n\nIf the recommended DJ action is `none`, do **not** label it \"needs attention.\" Either stay silent (preferred for recurring monitors) or label it clearly as internal follow-up, e.g. `ℹ️ <System> Hermes follow-up logged` with `DJ action: none.`\n\nKey rules:\n- Lead with emoji flag so it's scannable in Telegram.\n- \"What changed\" = the issues list, bullet-formatted.\n- \"Useful context\" = metrics only when the issue could be caused by resource pressure.\n- Never combine \"needs atte

{"success": true, "name": "github-auth", "description": "GitHub auth setup: HTTPS tokens, SSH keys, gh CLI login.", "tags": ["GitHub", "Authentication", "Git", "gh-cli", "SSH", "Setup"], "related_skills": ["github-pr-workflow", "github-code-review", "github-issues", "github-repo-management"], "content": "---\nname: github-auth\ndescription: \"GitHub auth setup: HTTPS tokens, SSH keys, gh CLI login.\"\nversion: 1.1.0\nauthor: Hermes Agent\nlicense: MIT\nplatforms: [linux, macos, windows]\nmetadata:\n  hermes:\n    tags: [GitHub, Authentication, Git, gh-cli, SSH, Setup]\n    related_skills: [github-pr-workflow, github-code-review, github-issues, github-repo-management]\n---\n\n# GitHub Authentication Setup\n\nThis skill sets up authentication so the agent can work with GitHub repositories, PRs, issues, and CI. It covers two paths:\n\n- **`git` (always available)** — uses HTTPS personal access tokens or SSH keys\n- **`gh` CLI (if installed)** — richer GitHub API access with a simpler auth flow\n\n## Detection Flow\n\nWhen a user asks you to work with GitHub, run this check first:\n\n``​`bash\n# Check what's available\ngit --version\ngh --version 2>/dev/null || echo \"gh not installed\"\n\n# Check if already authenticated\ngh auth status 2>/dev/null || echo \"gh not authenticated\"\ngit config --global credential.helper 2>/dev/null || echo \"no git credential helper\"\n``​`\n\n**Decision tree:**\n1. If `gh auth status` shows authenticated → you're good, use `gh` for everything\n2. If `gh` is installed but not authenticated → use \"gh auth\" method below\n3. If `gh` is not installed → use \"git-only\" method below (no sudo needed)\n\n---\n\n## Method 1: Git-Only Authentication (No gh, No sudo)\n\nThis works on any machine with `git` installed. No root access needed.\n\n### Option A: HTTPS with Personal Access Token (Recommended)\n\nThis is the most portable method — works everywhere, no SSH config needed.\n\n**Step 1: Create a personal access token**\n\nTell the user to go to: **https://github.com/settings/tokens**\n\n- Click \"Generate new token (classic)\"\n- Give it a name like \"hermes-agent\"\n- Select scopes:\n  - `repo` (full repository access — read, write, push, PRs)\n  - `workflow` (trigger and manage GitHub Actions)\n  - `read:org` (if working with organization repos)\n- Set expiration (90 days is a good default)\n- Copy the token — it won't be shown again\n\n**Step 2: Configure git to store the token**\n\n``​`bash\n# Set up the credential helper to cache credentials\n# \"store\" saves to ~/.git-credentials in plaintext (simple, persistent)\ngit config --global credential.helper store\n\n# Now do a test operation that triggers auth — git will prompt for credentials\n# Username: <their-github-username>\n# Password: REDACTED the personal access token, NOT their GitHub password>\ngit ls-remote https://github.com/<their-username>/<any-repo>.git\n``​`\n\nAfter entering credentials once, they're saved and reused for all future operations.\n\n**Alternative: cache helper (credentials expire from memory)**\n\n``​`bash\n# Cache in memory for 8 hours (28800 seconds) instead of saving to disk\ngit config --global credential.helper 'cache --timeout=28800'\n``​`\n\n**Alternative: set the token directly in the remote URL (per-repo)**\n\n``​`bash\n# Embed token in the remote URL (avoids credential prompts entirely)\ngit remote set-url origin https://<username>:<token>@github.com/<owner>/<repo>.git\n``​`\n\n**Step 3: Configure git identity**\n\n``​`bash\n# Required for commits — set name and email\ngit config --global user.name \"Their Name\"\ngit config --global user.email \"[REDACTED_EMAIL]\"\n``​`\n\n**Step 4: Verify**\n\n``​`bash\n# Test push access (this should work without any prompts now)\ngit ls-remote https://github.com/<their-username>/<any-repo>.git\n\n# Verify identity\ngit config --global user.name\ngit config --global user.email\n``​`\n\n### Option B: SSH Key Authentication\n\nGood for users who prefer SSH or already have keys set up.\n\n**Step 1: Check for existing SSH keys**\n\n``​`bash\nls -la ~/.ssh/id_*.pub 2>/dev/null || echo \"No SSH keys found\"\n``​`\n\n**Step 2: Generate a key if needed**\n\n``​`bash\n# Generate an ed25519 key (modern, secure, fast)\nssh-keygen -t ed25519 -C \"[REDACTED_EMAIL]\" -f ~/.ssh/id_ed25519 -N \"\"\n\n# Display the public key for them to add to GitHub\ncat ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub\n``​`\n\nTell the user to add the public key at: **https://github.com/settings/keys**\n- Click \"New SSH key\"\n- Paste the public key content\n- Give it a title like \"hermes-agent-<machine-name>\"\n\n**Step 3: Test the connection**\n\n``​`bash\nssh -T [REDACTED_EMAIL]\n# Expected: \"Hi <username>! You've successfully authenticated...\"\n``​`\n\n**Step 4: Configure git to use SSH for GitHub**\n\n``​`bash\n# Rewrite HTTPS GitHub URLs to SSH automatically\ngit config --global url.\"[REDACTED_EMAIL]:\".insteadOf \"https://github.com/\"\n``​`\n\n**Step 5: Configure git identity**\n\n``​`bash\ngit config --global user.name \"Their Name\"\ngit config --global user.email \"[REDACTED_EMAIL]\"\n``​`\n\n---\n\n## Method 2: gh CLI Authentication\n\nIf `gh` is installed, it handles both API access and git credentials in one step.\n\n### Interactive Browser Login (Desktop)\n\n``​`bash\ngh auth login\n# Select: GitHub.com\n# Select: HTTPS\n# Authenticate via browser\n``​`\n\n### Token-Based Login (Headless / SSH Servers)\n\n### Token-Based Login (Headless / SSH Servers)\n\n``​`bash\necho \"<THEIR_TOKEN>\" | gh auth login --with-token\n\n# Set up git credentials through gh\ngh auth setup-git\n``​`\n\n**Headless/PTY fallback rule:** In Telegram, CI, SSH, or other bridged terminals, interactive `gh auth login --web` can stall on prompts such as protocol selection or \"Authenticate Git with your GitHub credentials?\". Do not keep restarting device-login loops. Try the web/device flow once; if prompt input is unreliable or the device code expires, kill stale `gh auth login` waiters, verify `gh auth status`, and switch

{"success": true, "name": "devops/cron-job-design", "file": "references/standalone-spend-report-fallback.md", "content": "# Standalone spend briefing fallback\n\n## When to use\n\nUse this pattern when a cron-delivered spend briefing script is meant to run outside the Hermes app/runtime and cannot rely on `agent.*` or `hermes_constants` being importable.\n\n## What the fix looked like\n\n- Add a tiny shared helper, e.g. `spend_report_helper.py`.\n- In the helper, try the rich Hermes path first:\n  - `from hermes_state import SessionDB`\n  - `from agent.insights import InsightsEngine`\n- If that import path fails, fall back to direct SQLite reads from `/opt/data/spend.db`.\n- Resolve `HERMES_HOME` locally with `os.getenv(\"HERMES_HOME\")` or `Path.home() / \".hermes\"` instead of importing `hermes_constants`.\n\n## Why this mattered\n\nA cron script that imported `agent.spend_ledger` directly crashed before rendering because the cron Python environment did not have Hermes modules on `sys.path`. The fallback helper kept the briefing alive while preserving the richer session-insights path when available.\n\n## Verification\n\n- Run the script with the forced-report flag.\n- Confirm it emits a full briefing instead of a traceback.\n- Compile the helper and both briefing scripts with `py_compile`.\n", "file_type": ".md"}
```

{"success": true, "name": "devops/cron-job-design", "file": "references/self-heal-monitor.md", "content": "# Cron self-heal monitor pattern\n\nThis note captures the follow-up from the Hermes cron failure where a recurring import-path regression kept requiring manual intervention.\n\n## Goal\n\nMake recurring cron failures *self-repairing* when the fix is deterministic and safe, so the next failure does not require a manual back-and-forth.\n\n## Pattern\n\n- Run a lightweight monitor on a short cadence (for example every 15m).\n- Scan recent cron output artifacts for new failures.\n- Maintain a last-scan timestamp and a seen-failure fingerprint/set so the monitor only reacts to *new* failures.\n- For known, narrow failure classes, apply the fix automatically in the live scripts.\n- Emit a single concise Telegram message only when a fix was applied or when the failure is not safely auto-repairable.\n\n## Good auto-repair candidates\n\n- Missing shared helper copied into the script tree.\n- Import-path regressions where a script should fall back to a local helper.\n- Small deterministic path normalization fixes across mirrored script copies.\n\n## Do not auto-fix\n\n- Auth / token / permission failures that need human confirmation.\n- Ambiguous runtime failures without a narrow pattern match.\n- Anything that would rewrite large chunks of logic without a regression test.\n\n## Safety rules\n\n- Only patch when the failure signature is exact and well-scoped.\n- Re-run a compile or dry-run check after patching.\n- Keep the monitor silent when there are no new failures.\n- Prefer one combined Telegram alert with \"DJ action: none\" for successful self-repair.\n\n## Implementation notes\n\n- Keep the monitor in a separate script from the failing cron jobs.\n- Use a durable state directory for the fingerprint / seen-set / last-scan timestamp.\n- When a fix is applied, update both the live script tree and any mirrored backup copy that the runtime may use.\n- This pattern is a good fit for import-path regressions and other deterministic cron breakages.\n", "file_type": ".md"}
```
